- Already covered, so not added: tests kept away from the real config and Trash, fake clocks, the Time Machine and iCloud warnings, and the handling of disconnected drives.
- Not worth it here:
  - fingerprint caching by content (the gate's results are already remembered)
  - one read per file and per-drive read queues (scans are incremental, and the reads are small)
  - security-scoped bookmarks (yt-dlp's self-updates mean this is never a Mac App Store app)
- 1,103 tests pass.

### Step 09b: the calibration run (2026-09-30)

- The owner ran `plan replace --only auto --limit 25 --stage-only`, then `apply` and `queue run`:
  - All 25 downloads finished in about 15 minutes.
  - One HTTP 403 was retried a minute later and succeeded.
  - `plan calibration` wrote the pairs file.
- **Verdicts:**
  - 24 were `match`, with BER from 0.011 to 0.110. The limit for a match is 0.15.
  - 1 was `uncertain`: fpcalc couldn't read the rip.
- **Fixed, `fingerprint.py`:** that rip (an MP3) has a cut-off last frame.
  - fpcalc read it to the end but exited with "Invalid data found", and the engine threw the fingerprint away.
  - Now, when fpcalc fails but printed a fingerprint covering at least 90% of the file (less the ~2.7 s every whole-file fingerprint stops short of the end), that fingerprint is used and the damage is logged. The comparison is as strict as ever.
  - Damage earlier in a file still fails. fpcalc stops at the damage, and a test shows it.
  - With this fix, the rip compares with its download as `match` at BER 0.038: 25 of 25.
- **Different-version pairs, from the research's saved fingerprints** (read-only, outside the repo):
  - 32 of the owner's remix rips (an "R" at the end of the file name means remix, the owner's convention), each against the original of the same song. No audio was downloaded again.
  - `calibrate_fp.py` on the 57 pairs (25 same, 32 different):
    - Same: all 25 `match`. BER from 0.011 to 0.110 (median 0.046); at most 2 s of extra audio at the start and 3 s at the end.
    - Different: all 32 `different`. BER from 0.347 to 0.495.
    - **No different pair came near a match.**
  - The script suggests match ≤ 0.169 and uncertain ≤ 0.287. **Kept at 0.15 and 0.25** (config.json unchanged): every same pair already passes with room to spare, and the stricter values are the safer ones.
- **First real batch** (acceptance 4–6):
  - `plan replace --only auto --limit 25`, `apply`, `queue run`: 25 `done`; 2 network hiccups were retried and worked; 0 failed, 0 to review.
  - All 25 files have title, artist, album, year, track number and total, and full provenance.
  - Checked in Kid3, not Apple Music: the owner's rips live in Apple Music's own media folder, so adding the new files there would list every song twice. Cover art arrives with step 10.
  - `undo` put the library back exactly: `Music/` empty, the 25 files in `_Replaced/`, `matched_auto` back to 735 (710 + 25), and no superseded links left.
  - The undo printout no longer lists the tag writes done in staging (still in `--json`).
- A new plan after the undo (the end of acceptance 6): 25 `done`, 0 failed, 0 to review. Two HTTP 403s were retried and worked.
  - All 25 files are complete and linked (25 superseded), and they stay in the library.
  - Downloads in the last 24 hours: 80 of 300.
- **Step 09b's acceptance passes.** Bigger batches come next, with the daily cap left on (acceptance 7).

### Step 09c: Keep your own audio (the owner's decision, 2026-09-30)

- **Why:** the owner already has about 1,800 songs and doesn't want them downloaded again. Downloading stays for people moving off streaming services, and for a song the owner picks.

  - That batch was undone; its files are in `_Replaced/`.
- `plan adopt --matched` copies in `matched_auto` and `matched_user` rips, keeping their audio, with their match's official details. Nothing is downloaded. See `prompts/09c-keep-your-own-audio.md`.
  - Tags: title, artist, album artist, album, year, track number and total, and explicit.
  - A track number, total or disc that YouTube Music doesn't give is removed, because the rip's may belong to another CD. Other tags, like genre, stay.
  - Provenance: `SOURCE=rip_copy`, `SOURCE_ID` = the match's videoId, and `MATCH=auto_details` or `user_details` (new values in `ENGINE_API.md` → Enums and `tags.MATCHES`). No `ONLY_COPY`.
  - Left out, and counted in the summary: matches the fingerprint gate turned down, and WebM, raw AAC or WAV rips, which only a download would fix.
  - The next plan picks up songs matched or confirmed later.
- `youtube.get_album(…, cache=index)`: album answers are kept in the index for 30 days, so songs from one album ask once. Only the fields the engine uses are kept, plus thumbnails for step 10. Replace jobs use the cache too.
- The contract's format table, `MUSICORG_SOURCE_ID` and `MUSICORG_ONLY_COPY` describe the new kind of file.
- New tests:
  - the official details applied and the audio untouched
  - the rip's disc and track numbers removed when they don't belong
  - its genre kept
  - one album lookup shared by two songs
  - no download ever
  - undo back to `matched_auto` and `matched_user`
  - turned-down and WebM matches left out
- **Acceptance run on the owner's library** (2026-09-30): `plan adopt --matched` → `apply` → `queue run`.
  - 817 of 817 done (735 `auto_details`, 82 `user_details`), 0 failed, no downloads. Faster than the 55 min estimate, because songs share album lookups.
  - 352 artist folders; about 4.2 GB copied; every rip untouched.
  - 6 files have no track number and 2 no album or year, because YouTube Music couldn't place them. They're left empty rather than guessed.

### Step 10: Lyrics and covers

- `lyrics.py`: LRCLIB first, then YouTube Music. The owner asked for every source that allows it; Genius and Musixmatch's own API need paid keys or forbid automatic copying.
  - **LRCLIB:** `/api/get` (album left out when unknown or `Unsorted`), then `/api/search`. A search result counts only within 2 s and with the same artist and title. At most one request a second, with a User-Agent naming the project. Checked against the research's 1,357 saved answers, since LRCLIB's docs page renders only in a browser.
  - **YouTube Music:** `youtube.get_lyrics`, via `get_watch_playlist()["lyrics"]` then `get_lyrics(browseId, timestamps=True)`. Checked live with ytmusicapi 1.12.3: timed lines with `start_time` in ms, from Musixmatch or LyricFind.
  - **Guards:**
    - version: a remix or live track whose LRCLIB title lacks that word gets plain lyrics only (`version_uncertain`)
    - timing (new): synced lyrics only when the file is within 2 s of the length they were timed for. The owner's own audio (09c) can have a video intro.
    - every LRC text is checked
  - Answers, "not found" included, are cached in the index for 30 days.
- `artwork.py`: the album's cover at 1200 px, or the owner's `art_url`.
  - Checked live: an album listed at 544 px comes at 1200 px, and asking for more than the original returns the original (1425 px), never an enlarged copy.
  - Square within 2% and at least 500 px, else kept anyway and logged. JPEG quality 90 at most 1200 px, made in memory; a JPEG already 1200 px or less is never re-encoded.
  - An album's cover is fetched once per run. Songs with no official match get nothing automatic.
- `youtube.py`:
  - `get_lyrics`, `fetch_image` (https only; a 404 is an error, not a slow-down), `page_thumbnail` (yt-dlp, no download), `sized_thumbnail`
  - `Album.thumbnails`, which the album cache already keeps
  - all through the shared limiter
- `pipeline.py`:
  - **New replaces and adopts get lyrics and a cover in the same verified tag write** (`EXTRAS`: `lyrics_extras`, `cover_extras`).
  - `musicorg lyrics [--missing]` and `musicorg artwork [--missing]` plan the same for songs already in the library, one job per song, and `apply` queues them.
  - `artwork --missing` also takes songs whose cover isn't square: a converter's 16:9 video frame.
  - Undo takes both back: the tags return to how they were, and the `.lrc` and `cover.jpg` go to `_Replaced/`.
- `review`: `art_url` is read from the spreadsheet and the review page, for only-copy rows, https only.
- **Deviations:**
  - **Lyrics and cover jobs aren't "network jobs" in the queue's sense.** They download no audio, so they don't wait the 8–25 s download pace or count toward the 300-a-day cap. LRCLIB's one-a-second pace and YouTube's shared limiter set their speed. The brief called them network jobs; taken literally, 817 songs would take hours and use up the day's downloads.
  - **Lyrics and cover jobs check the file's identity, not its bytes.** It must still be there with the same MUSICORG_ID. These jobs only add their own fields in a verified write, so a lyrics batch before an artwork one doesn't make the artwork plan stale.
  - **No new index columns** for "instrumental" or "version uncertain" (a schema change would force a rebuild, losing the review candidates). The 30-day answer cache stops repeat lookups, and each job's result line says what was found.
- **Tests never contain real lyrics or covers:** lyrics are copyrighted and the repo is public. The recordings keep LRCLIB's and YouTube Music's real shapes, with made-up lines. Covers are made in memory.
- Also fixed: `queue.run(kinds={})` fell back to every registered kind.
- **Acceptance run on the owner's library** (2026-09-30): `lyrics --missing` (807 songs; 10 already had lyrics) and `artwork --missing` (787 songs; 28 had a square cover already, 2 have no official album). Both applied, then one `queue run`: 1,594 jobs done, 0 failed, 0 to review; 2 network hiccups were retried and worked.
  - Lyrics:
    - **708 synced** (88%): 634 from LRCLIB and 74 from YouTube Music (Musixmatch or LyricFind), so the second source added 74 songs
    - 55 plain only, 19 of them because of the timing guard (the file 2–3 s off the length the lyrics were timed for)
    - 12 instrumental
    - 32 not found
    - The version guard didn't trigger.
  - Covers: 787 embedded and 505 `cover.jpg` files; 0 failed. Most are 1200 × 1200. 15 albums only offer 512 px. 30 official covers aren't square within 2% (e.g. 1145 × 1200); they're kept and logged.
  - Checks: every "synced" song has its `.lrc` next to it. The lyrics' tag writes (plain lyrics, in 763 songs) and the covers' (787) all passed the verified-write check, where the decoded audio must be unchanged.
- **Step 10's acceptance:** the counts above are recorded. What's left is the owner's check of a few `.lrc` files against playback, in a player that reads them.

### Step 11: The JSON-RPC server (`musicorg serve`)

- `rpc.py` implements every method, notification and error in `ENGINE_API.md` section 2.
  - **Transport:**
    - Newline-delimited JSON on stdin and stdout, in binary mode.
    - `protect_stdout()` duplicates descriptor 1 for the protocol, then points 1 at stderr, so a child process (ffmpeg, fpcalc, deno) or a stray print can't corrupt the stream.
    - Deviation: the brief's `os.fdopen(os.dup(1), 'wb')` is written as `os.write` on the duplicated descriptor (`ProtocolOut`). The write-rules test rightly flags any `fdopen` for writing outside fileops, and the protocol stream isn't a file on disk. The effect is the same, and CLAUDE.md's rule 3 needed no new exception.
    - One writer lock for every message.
  - **Lifecycle:**
    - `engine.hello` comes first.
    - `library.open` holds the lock for the engine's lifetime.
    - On stdin EOF (and SIGTERM on macOS), no new work is taken, the queue's job gets 10 s, and the lock is released, exit 0. A job still running after 10 s is queued again at the next start; the crash-loop guard caps that.
  - **Workers:**
    - The queue worker runs `queue.run` from `library.open`, and again after `plan.apply` or `queue.resume`.
    - One long operation at a time (`sources.scan`, `match.run`, a non-dry-run `journal.undo`) returns `{job_id}`. Its progress goes out at most 4 times a second, the last always, and it ends with `job.finished`. A second one gets -32007.
  - **Errors:** every engine exception maps to a code, and an unexpected one to -32603 with the log's path.
- `scripts/rpc_client.py` plays the app's part:
  - makes a scratch library with five made-up rips named after recorded matcher cases
  - runs hello, open, status, `sources.add`, a scan and `match.run` (limit 5) in replay mode, printing every notification
  - closes stdin and checks for exit code 0
  - It runs in the test suite too, so the Windows runner exercises it.
- `state.edit()` holds a lock, so a queue job and an app request never interleave their state.json edits.
- 19 new tests:
  - the handshake
  - every protocol error, and the engine errors' codes
  - every method's shape, on a real scratch library
  - notifications during a job, and throttled progress
  - the busy error
  - 1,000 notifications from four threads, every line whole
  - `musicorg serve` as a real process: a second engine refused with -32001, a clean exit on EOF and on SIGTERM, the lock released
  - a child process writing to descriptor 1 without breaking the stream
  - the no-print rule: only `cli.py` calls `print`
  - the API doc naming every method
- **Doc changes** (`ENGINE_API.md`, to match the implementation):
  - -32000 for a request that can't be done, with a plain-English reason. The doc had no code for ordinary user errors.
  - The defaults: `review.list` limit 50, at most 500; `journal.undo` a dry run unless `dry_run` is false; `search.ytmusic` limit 10.
  - `plan.create`'s kinds and options, including 09c's `matched` and step 10's `missing`.
  - When `queue.state` and `library.changed` are sent.
- `musicorg serve` replaced the last "not implemented yet" stub, and `NotImplementedYetError` is gone.

2026-10-08, about 1:05pm. **A phone can carry on a file that was cut off.** With films in the share, fetching one from the start after a dropped connection costs minutes. `GET /sync/v1/files/<id>` now answers `Range: bytes=<from>-` (or `<from>-<to>`) with 206 and only those bytes, for a file on disk and for a cover or lyrics kept inside a song's tags alike. A phone sends `If-Range` with the version it has the start of; if the file has another version now it's sent whole, so new bytes are never joined to old. A part that isn't in the file is 416; any other kind of `Range` gets the whole file. Every file answer carries its version as `ETag`. Format 1 still: a phone that never sends `Range` sees no difference. It was on the "Not in format 1" list and in CLAUDE.md's "not yet"; both are updated. Nothing in the app changed.

2026-10-08, about 1:25pm. **A movie that's already kept can be converted for phones and tablets.** Until now only a film converted as it was kept came out as one a phone plays; an MKV already in the Movies folder stayed out of the share. `media.convert` takes the path of a real file inside the Movies folder or the videos folder (not a link, nothing anywhere else) and, if `convert.plan` says it needs it, makes the copy the same way a kept film's is made: ffmpeg writes it in the films' cache folder, `fileops.keep_media` puts it **beside the film** as `<its name>.mp4` (a number after it if that's taken), and the cache copy is cleared. **The film itself is only read and stays where it is**: the engine never deletes outside the library, so the owner removes the original once the copy plays. One at a time; `media.converting` says how far along it is and how the last one ended, and `media.converted` is sent when it ends. New in `fileops`: `make_cached_folder` (the films' cache folder may not exist if no torrent was ever played). No contract change: this is the kept-media writer doing what section 1 already allows, from the app's cache into the folder the film is in. App: Convert for Phones and Tablets in a video file's right-click menu (Videos → Movies, and the downloaded videos under Videos → Channel), with "Converting… 42%" and how it ended beside the file's kind. Proven by tests that run ffmpeg for real on a small MKV; **not seen in the running app** (a right-click menu can't be opened from here).

2026-10-08, about 1:45pm. **A film being kept carries on when the app is reopened, and a keep can be stopped.** A keep ran only while the engine did: closing the app part-way meant finding the film and clicking Keep again. The app now remembers each keep that isn't finished (`PendingKeeps` in the Kit, kept in the app's own settings on this Mac, not in the library and not by the engine) and asks `torrent.keep` for each again when it opens; what had arrived is still in the cache if it's within the day, so it goes on from there. One is forgotten when its film is in the Movies folder, couldn't be put there, or is stopped. **The torrent promise says so now:** a torrent is joined only from the owner's click, and the click on Keep is the one that carries a film over a restart. Because a keep now outlives the app, it needs a way to end: `torrent.stop_keeping` (the torrent is left unless the film is playing; nothing in the Movies folder is touched; one already being written there is finished), and the Keep button reads Stop Keeping while a film is on its way. A carried-on keep that can't start (no network yet) says nothing and is tried at the next opening. Run against a real public-domain torrent in a scratch folder (keep, stop, keep again, stop): the first run showed a stopped keep staying joined ten minutes more, because a film that's only being kept is marked as playing too; the app now says whether the film is in its player (`playing`), and otherwise the torrent is left at once. The second keep went on from what the first had fetched. **Not seen in the app.**

2026-10-08, about 2:10pm. **How fast torrents send on what they've fetched can be limited.** The owner: a torrent offers what it has to others, and Settings should limit that: Max, 5, 3, 1, or None. `settings.set` takes `torrent_upload` (a new enum, **Torrent upload**: `max`, `5`, `3`, `1`, `none`), kept in `config.json`; `torrents.upload_settings` turns it into libtorrent's own settings: a rate in bytes a second over every torrent together, and for `none` no turn for any other computer (`unchoke_slots_limit` 0), so nothing is sent. It applies at once to films joined now, and a new session starts with it. `max`, the standard, is what happened before. The numbers are megabytes a second (the unit the app shows a film's speed in); the owner wrote "Mb", so megabits may have been meant: to be confirmed. App: a five-way picker under Settings → Downloads → Movies and Videos. Checked against libtorrent 2.1.1's setting names; **not measured on a real torrent** (that needs a film with others asking for it).

2026-10-08, about 2:20pm. **Settings asked for while the library is still being read opens when it's ready.** Found looking at the new setting: Settings chosen from the menu during "Reading your library…" did nothing, and then nothing opened it for the rest of that run (the menu, the gear, an Open Settings… button), because the page asked for was left waiting and asking for the same page again changed nothing. The main view now also looks at what's waiting when it first appears.

It gives lists (trending, top airing, most popular, highest rated and more, by genre) and details of anime, and nothing to play: the same standing as the film-details add-on. Checked against the live add-on and recorded (its manifest, one list, one series): its lists are type `anime`, what's in them is a `series` or `movie` with an id `kitsu:<n>`, and a series' details carry episodes by season like any other, so they open on the pages there already are. Three things came with it:

CLAUDE.md's add-on rule says four starting add-ons now.

2026-10-08, about 4:30pm. **Add-ons for adults only are fenced into a Finder of their own.** The owner asked for one, "subject to deletion if it doesn't work out", never in a child's profile, never on Home. No such add-on is built in or tested against (the tests use a made-up one); the owner adds one by its address, as any other.

- **What counts:** an add-on whose manifest says `behaviorHints.adult`, or one the owner marks in Settings → Add-ons (right-click → Mark as Adults Only; `addon.mark`). An Addon carries `adult`.
- **Where it shows:** every list of such an add-on, of whatever type, in the last Finder of the sidebar, and nowhere else. `MediaBrowser.catalogs(of:)`, which Home and every other Finder read their lists from, leaves those add-ons out. A title opened there has no favourite heart and isn't remembered as watched, so nothing of it reaches Home's rows. The row can be taken out in Customise Sidebar like any other.
- **A child's profile:** the engine is told it's a child's (`kids.set`) and from then that profile's list has no adults-only add-on in it: nothing can be asked of one, one can't be added, a mark can't be changed. The list of add-ons is one for the whole computer, so changes made in a child's profile (remove, reorder, put back) are made to the whole kept list and leave the unseen ones where they were. The app also hides the row and the page there, and reads the add-ons afresh when the profile changes (it used to keep the last profile's).
- An adults-only add-on keeps every genre it offers; the five taken off other add-ons' lists still are.
- **Not fenced:** if such an add-on also offers ways to play an ordinary film (by its `tt` id), they'd be listed on that film's page with the others. A film kept from it goes to the Movies folder like any kept film, and from there into Videos → Movies and, with that switch on, the share.

2026-10-08, about 5:30pm. **Music Finder → Playlists follows what's being listened to, and doesn't repeat itself.** The owner asked for a page that "would refresh with recently played and always be evolving to not show the same playlists over and over"; as first built it changed only with the day and the Different Playlists button. Now: the two songs played last are always among the songs playlists are looked for around (`discover.playlists`, `NEWEST_FIRST`); the app's word for the shuffle includes what was played last (`Listening.playedStamp`), and the tab asks again when that has changed since its page was found, so a song played through moves the page on the next time the tab is on screen; and the playlists shown lately (`PlaylistsSeen`, the last 120, kept by the app on this Mac) are left out of what's asked for, until there's nothing else, when they come round again. Each refresh is at most six searches, kept 30 days by their words, and only while the tab is open. Not watched over a listening session in the running app.

2026-10-08, about 5:50pm. **The adults-only fence covers details and ways to play.** Listed as not fenced when the Finder was built: an add-on for adults that also answered for an ordinary film's id would have had its ways to play listed on that film's page. `addon.details` and `addon.streams` now ask one side or the other (`addons.kept_to`): the page for adults says `adult: true` and is answered only by those add-ons; every other page never asks one. Still as before: a title kept from that Finder goes to the Movies folder like any kept film.

2026-10-08, about 6:15pm. **Kept series and anime have folders of their own, and the home share says which is which.** The owner asked for a phone player to have the app's own sections ("we need to have Movies, Series, Anime"). Until now nothing recorded what a kept file was: an episode was one more file in the Movies folder, and the share's list sent everything there as a `movie`. Asked how it should be recorded, the owner chose the folder a file is kept in (over a note beside the app's settings) and said yes to the contract change that goes with it.

- **Contract, section 1, changed with the owner's yes.** A series' episode is kept as `Series/<Show>/Season 1/<Show> S01E02 - <Episode Title>` inside the Movies folder, an anime's the same way under `Anime/`, and an anime that's a film as `Anime/<Title> (<Year>)`. A film stays loose in the Movies folder, as before. The folder is the only record of what a file is: it doesn't depend on the app's settings, a converted copy (which goes beside its film) is filed with it, and a file the owner puts in those folders themselves is filed the same way.
- **The rule is in `naming`:** `kept_place` (where a kept film or episode goes) and `kept_details` (what a file is, read back from its path). `torrent.keep` takes `kind` (a new enum, **Kept kind**), `show`, `season` and `episode`. `fileops.keep_media` didn't change: it already made a missing folder inside the Movies folder, and it still never overwrites.
- **Home sync, still format 1** (the new keys are extra ones): every entry of `movies` has `kind` (`movie`, `series`, `anime` or `video`), and an episode has `show` and, where its name or folder says them, `season` and `episode`. Its `title` is the episode's own when the file's name gives one. Of where a file is kept, the show's name is all that's sent. `sharing.kept_media` keeps every guard it had (real files inside the two folders, no link followed, hidden files and other programs' folders left alone) and still writes nothing; a film's id is the one it had.
- **The app:** Keep on an episode sends its show, season, episode and title. What's opened from Anime Finder is marked as anime (`MediaItem.asAnime`: the engine's item only says "series" or "movie"), and a favourite or what's been watched carries the mark, so it's still anime when it's opened from Home. No screen looks any different.
- **Only what's known is written or sent.** An episode with no title of its own is `<Show> S01E02`; one whose season isn't known is `<Show>/<Show> E02`; season 0 is the folder `Specials`. An anime's numbers are the add-on's own: Anime Kitsu lists each season as a show of its own, so "<Show> 2" is its own row with a season 1, and nothing is renumbered.
- **Films already kept stay movies** (the owner's choice). Nothing is guessed from a file's name, and the engine never moves or renames a kept file. An episode kept before today (loose, as `<Show> S01E02`) is filed as a series once the owner moves it into `Series/<Show>/` themselves. A kept file's id goes by where it is, so to a phone a moved file is a new one.
- **Windows:** a kept path is fitted into 259 characters. A show's name is cut by a rule that goes only by the Movies folder, so a show can't end up with two folders.
- **Downloads' rows for movies being kept** (added on main the same evening, joined with this when the two were merged) name an episode by its show and number, and say Series or Anime where a film's says Movie. A keep also still carries main's mark for the Finder for adults. What's kept from that Finder is filed by what it is, like anything else kept.
- **One gap:** an anime made a favourite, or watched, before today has no mark. Kept after being opened from Home it's filed as a series or a movie; opened from Anime Finder it's filed right.
- **Tried for real, with made-up files in a scratch folder:** episodes kept through the engine's own keep path (an MKV made into an MP4 on the way) landed in their shows' folders; the list fetched over HTTP on this computer named each one's kind, show, season and episode, and a fetched episode was the kept file byte for byte; an episode kept as an MKV wasn't listed, and a loose `<Show> S01E03` stayed a movie. The app's own Movies page lists them all, folders and all (it walks the folders inside the Movies folder).
- **Not tried:** a real episode from a torrent, and the app's Keep button on one. None of the app's own add-ons has a series or an anime to play, none that does is tested against (CLAUDE.md), and a torrent is only ever joined from the owner's click. What the button sends is covered by the Kit's test.

2026-10-08, about 6:20pm. **Movies being kept are listed on Downloads.** A keep showed only on its movie's own page, so one carried on from the last time the app was open was out of sight. Downloads now lists them above the songs and videos on their way, in the same kind of row: the movie's name, a bar for how much has arrived (then for the converting), the line its own page shows, and ✕ to stop it; one that has been kept, or couldn't be, stays until it's taken off the list. They count in the number on Downloads' row in the sidebar. A movie kept from the Finder for adults isn't listed there (`PendingKeeps.Keep.apart`): that page's things stay on that page. App only; the rows are the existing ones' layout, not a new screen. **Not seen with a real keep in the running app.**

2026-10-08, about 6:25pm. **The roadmap has a section for videos, movies and series.** It had none: the work began after it was written. A short one now says what's built, what waits on the owner, and what isn't built, and points at CLAUDE.md and this file for the rest. Docs only.

2026-10-08, about 6:40pm. **Pushed, and CI asked for again.** The day's commits went to GitHub at about 6:15pm, but no CI run started for them (the push printed "cannot lock ref" though the branch had moved: it looks to have been sent twice, and the run's trigger was lost; GitHub reported no outage). This entry is the commit that asks for the run. Docs only.

2026-10-08, about 7:30pm. **A found playlist opens where it was found, and downloads nothing.** The owner: clicking a playlist under Music Finder → Playlists "should show the playlist, offer to play all the songs in that playlist, and the typical queue download". It used to go to Import Playlists, whose page is for bringing a whole playlist across (it begins finding the songs for download at once, with Make the Playlist as its one button). Now the playlist opens in the tab: its name, who made it and how many songs; Play All; and each song in the row every found song has (play, Queue, Download, the artist's page). Back returns to the playlists. Import as a Playlist… is still there as a button for bringing the whole thing across. The playlist is read with the same `import.playlist` as before: no engine change. **Not seen in the running app** (the owner was using it).

2026-10-08, about 8:15pm. **Songs aren't doubled in a playlist without being asked, and a copied playlist uses the songs the other profile already has.** The owner had four songs in a playlist, added every song in Songs to it, and had those four twice.

- **Adding to a playlist** (the app): if the playlist has some of the songs already, it asks first: "4 of these 1886 songs are already in “Road Trip”", with Skip Them (only the ones not there go in), Add Again (all of them, as before) and Cancel. When every song is there already the choice is Add Again or Don't Add. A song picked twice in one go counts once. (`Playlist.sorting(adding:)` and `alreadyQuestion` in the Kit.)
- **Copy to Profile** (the engine's `share` plan): a song the other library had was recognised only by its own id or its YouTube id, so two people who had each put the same song in their own library ended up with it twice. It's now also recognised by title, version and first artist together, when exactly one song there answers to them: the playlist is given that library's own copy, and nothing is copied. A remix isn't taken for the original, a same-named song by someone else isn't taken for it, and if two songs there answer, it's copied as before, since it isn't clear which was meant. The note shown when the playlist arrives says how many were there already.

Not seen in the running app.

2026-10-08, about 8:30pm. **Remove Duplicates, in a playlist's right-click menu.** For the playlists doubled before the app asked: the item says how many there are ("Remove 4 Duplicates"), keeps each song once where it first comes, and says what it did. Only the list changes. It's greyed out when a playlist has none. App only (`Playlist.withoutDuplicates`); the engine's `playlist.set_tracks` does the saving, as for any change to a playlist.

2026-10-08, about 9:05pm. **One song the service won't hand over no longer pauses every download for six hours.** Found from the owner's evening: a song downloaded from the app was refused (HTTP 403) at 1:57pm, its retry a minute later was refused, and the first download of a `queue run` at 2:06pm was refused too; that was "3 downloads in a row", and everything waited until 8:06pm. When the pause ended the songs came down normally, with a stray 403 among them, as on 5 and 7 October. The count was of failures, and a job's retry was another failure, so one stubborn song could use up all three. **Changed with the owner's yes** (it's one of step 09a's limits): the queue now counts *different* downloads that have failed on the network since the last thing that went right (`network_failures_in_a_row` in queue.sqlite holds which, not how many). Three different ones in a row still pause the queue for six hours; one song refused again and again counts once, and goes on to its own retries and, in the end, `failed`. What YouTube says outright ("confirm you're not a bot", HTTP 429) pauses at once, as before. The message reads "3 different downloads in a row failed on the network". The cost: if YouTube really is refusing this computer, the pause begins a download or two later than it did.

2026-10-08, about 9:30pm. **No gap under the search bar when a search finds nothing.** The owner's picture: Downloads with a search that matched nothing showed its heading half-way down the window, under a wide empty space. The "no results" message kept its own small height, so the whole page was shorter than the window and was set in the middle of it. That message now fills the page (Downloads, the song lists, Albums), and every page is set at the top of its room, so a short page can't drift to the middle again whatever it holds.

2026-10-08, about 9:45pm. **Known issues lists what was built today and not yet seen working.** Ten things, each tested but not watched in the running app, in a section of their own in `docs/KNOWN-ISSUES.md`, with the questions still waiting on the owner. Also for the record: at 9:26pm YouTube answered a download with its "confirm you're not a bot" check and the queue paused for six hours, as it's meant to. What set it off isn't known; that day had 34 downloads, a library clean-up run, and many look-ups, the new tabs' among them. Docs only.


2026-10-08, about 11:15pm. **The app no longer gets slower the longer it's used, and a video is asked for once, not twice.** The owner: "It is extremely slow. Sometimes it takes several clicks to select anything. Searching takes a long time, to even be able to type anything", slow to come back from behind another window or from the Dock, slow in Settings, "it starts getting slow as you move toward media discovery". An audit, measured on the owner's library (1,888 songs) before anything was changed.

- **What it was.** Every page opened since the app started was kept inside the one window, the ones not showing moved far off to the side, so each came back exactly as it was left. Out of sight they were still part of the window: every click, every letter typed and every change of the window made macOS lay out all of them. A profile of the owner's copy after an hour's use: five clicks on the sidebar kept the app busy for 22 of 25 seconds, nearly all of it in SwiftUI's and AppKit's own layout, under 2% in the app's code. One click between two song lists cost about half a second in an app just opened, and many times that after an hour; one change to the search box, a second and a half and up. The Media Discovery pages (a grid of fifty cards, rows of posters) are the heaviest, which is why it got worse there.
- **Each page is now a view of its own** (`PageHost`, `PageStore`). The page showing is in the window; every other page waits in a window that's never put on the screen, exactly as it was left (scroll place, selection, sort, what was typed), where nothing lays it out, draws it or tells it the window changed. Putting it back is one step however much is on it. A page told it isn't showing stops working things out, so a search typed on one list isn't run on five. Taken out of every window instead (tried, and a little faster), a page counted as closed and asked the add-ons and the music service again each time it was shown: not acceptable, so they're parked.
- **Measured, same steps, same library**, with the new `MUSICORG_BENCH` (the app choosing the pages itself, so nothing else is in the timing), the old arrangement against the new:
  - A song list to another song list: 0.38 s, climbing to 0.55 s over six more pages opened (and to seconds over an hour). Now 0.30 to 0.45 s, the same with eleven pages open as with two.
  - Back to a page already opened: Home 0.44 s then, 0.13 s now; Music Finder 0.44 s then, 0.16 to 0.31 s now; Settings 0.12 s now.
  - A page for the first time: Music Finder 1.55 s then, 0.97 s now; Home 0.80 then, 0.47 now; Movie Finder 0.85 then, 0.48 now; Series Finder 0.96 then, 0.41 now.
  - The window hidden: 0.92 s then, 0.41 s now. Shown again: 0.87 then, 0.40 now. Put in the Dock: 1.28 then, 0.78 now, and the same again to bring it back (1.28 then, 0.74 now). These are with a song list showing, in an app just opened: a song table's three hundred cells are each told about the window, which is most of what's left.
  - A change to the library search: 1.44 s then, 0.78 s now.
  - A letter typed in Music Finder's search box: 0.01 s (it was never slow by itself: it was every page behind it being laid out).
  - Clicks made through the screen-control tools read 0.1 to 0.25 s higher than these, old and new alike: the tools ask the app about its window before each one.
- **Typing in the library search** shows each letter at once; the lists are searched when the typing has paused for a quarter of a second (or at Return), not at every letter.
- **A song list shown again** doesn't hand its table the same rows a second time.
- **An album** is opened by the window when it's clicked on the Albums page (`AppModel.openedAlbum`): a page that's a view of its own can't put another page over itself.
- **`MUSICORG_BENCH`** (a developer's check, like `MUSICORG_STALLS`): the app takes a list of steps by itself (pages, a search, hide and show) and writes how long it couldn't answer after each. `MUSICORG_PAGES=stacked` runs the old arrangement, to measure against.
- **One look-up for a video** (engine, `youtube._looked_up`; `fresh` on `youtube.stream` and `youtube.video`). A video played as itself (from a channel, Video Finder, a trailer) was asked about twice, once for its sound and once for its picture, one request after the other behind the rate limiter: the picture waited for both, five to ten seconds each. A video YouTube was asked about in the last five minutes is now answered from that, and a look-up already under way is waited for instead of made again; the app says `fresh` when an address has stopped working. Fewer requests reach YouTube, never more. Not timed against YouTube: it had this Mac paused that evening, so nothing was asked of it.

- **A song's "added" day is worked out once**, not at every showing of its row: reading the time afresh each time was a fifth of a second in every eight showings of a song list.

What's still slow, and why, is in `docs/KNOWN-ISSUES.md` ("The speed audit"): the song table itself.

2026-10-09, about 1:45am. **The song lists have a new table, made with AppKit's own.** The owner's yes to the mend the speed audit named ("rebuild the song table"). SwiftUI's `Table` did work for every song in a list, not only the ones on screen, and each of its cells was a small SwiftUI view of its own, some three hundred and fifty to a screen, every one of them told about each change of the window.

- **`SongTable`** (the app, `SongTable.swift`): an `NSTableView` that makes only the rows on screen and uses them again as the list scrolls. `SongList` still owns the list (which songs, their order, the selection, the sort); the table shows it. Made to look and work as the other did:
  - the same columns at the same widths, the title taking what the others leave; a click on a heading sorts, and a second turns it round;
  - View → Columns chooses the columns as before (the same saved choice), and so does a right-click on the headings; a column dragged to another place stays there for every list (`songColumnOrder`), the heart always first;
  - the same right-click menu on a song (SwiftUI's own description of it, shown through `NSHostingMenu`; before macOS 14.4, which can't do that, the menu is Play alone), a double click or Return to play, the heart to click, the cover, the badges, and the speaker on the song that's playing;
  - a download dragged onto the sidebar carries its id, as before.
- **Checked against the old table in pictures** of the same list at the same window size, in both looks (an unseen copy of the app on a made-up library of 1,675 songs): of three million dots, all but 361 are the same to the eye; those are two badges half a point to the left. Checked working there too: sorting, selecting several, a column switched on from the headings, the right-click menu's items for one song and for two, the heart, a search, and the selection coming back when the search is cleared.
- **Measured on the owner's library** (1,888 songs) in the window in front, `MUSICORG_BENCH`, old table then new: one song list to another, 0.40 s then 0.16 s; a jump to a fresh screenful, 0.22 to 0.33 s then 0.06 to 0.12 s; a search, 0.69 s then 0.38 s; the search cleared, 0.95 s then 0.29 s; the window hidden, 0.40 s then 0.16 s, and shown again, 0.43 s then 0.21 s. Putting the window in the Dock is as it was (about 0.8 s): that isn't the table. (In the unseen copy on the made-up library, a list opened for the first time in 0.22 s where the old table took 0.72 s.)
- **What made a screenful of new rows dear at first, and was mended the same night** (a profile of the new table: 0.17 to 0.36 s a screenful, most of it drawing): each cover was unpacked from its file on the main thread the first time it was drawn (they're unpacked where they're read now, off it: `Covers`, which helps every page that shows covers); each cell was drawn into a picture of its own (a row's cells are drawn into the row's one picture now); every badge kept a tip for the pointer whether it showed or not; and sizes that never change were measured at every layout.
- **Two small differences, said plainly.** A right-click on a song that isn't selected selects it (SwiftUI's table left the selection alone and drew a ring round the one clicked). Typing a song's first letters goes to it, which the old table didn't do.
- **View → Old Song Table** in the menu bar puts SwiftUI's table back, to look at beside the new one; it's off as standard. It's the one thing added to what the owner sees, and it's in the menu bar, not on a page.
- **`MUSICORG_BENCH`** learned to work the table as clicks would (`sort=`, `select=`, `column=`, `menu=`, `heart=`, `scroll=`, `drag=`), and `MUSICORG_SNAPSHOT_QUIET=1` runs the unseen copy without taking pictures.
- **Seen in the app itself** once the owner said it could be restarted: the Songs list with the owner's own columns, a row selected, a double click playing the song, and that row's speaker and heavier title, changing when it's paused. Not seen: a drag onto the sidebar, the menu opened by hand, a column dragged to a new place.

2026-10-09, about 2:15am. **The crash the owner had at 1:52am was the old song table, and a page no longer shows through another just after the app opens.**

- **The crash** ("quit unexpectedly", while the app was still the build from before the new table). The report: macOS's table was having its row height set by SwiftUI while its count of rows was out of date, asked SwiftUI about a row that was no longer there, and the app stopped on an array read past its end. The same crash, by the same path, is in three reports from 5 October. It belongs to SwiftUI's `Table` with a row height held against its will (`FixedRows`, which the old song table needed): SwiftUI puts its own height back at an update, and when the list has just got shorter in that same update, macOS lays the table out with the old count. The new table has neither part: it tells macOS about its rows itself, list first. It can still happen with View → Old Song Table switched on.
- **A second "quit unexpectedly" at 1:36am was mine**: the unseen test copy, when a test step sorted SwiftUI's table in a way it doesn't allow. The owner's app and library weren't involved. The step now leaves the old table alone.
- **A page being made ready showed through the page on screen** for the first twenty seconds after the app opened, in the Warm Look (where a page has no background of its own): the lists built ahead (`warmUp`) were put under the page showing. That came in with the pages' change of 8 October, and wasn't seen then because the app was looked at after it had settled. A click on an empty part of the page above could have landed on the one beneath. They're built in a part of the window that's never seen or clicked now (`PageStore.backstage`).

2026-10-09, about 2:40am. **The old song table is gone, the app no longer builds lists ahead when it opens, and a cover no longer looks on the disk from the main thread.** The owner: "remove old table and proceed", then "move onto the next 2 fixes… fixing what is needed".

- **SwiftUI's song table is taken out**, with View → Old Song Table, the helper that held its row height (`FixedRows`) and the sums that worked out its title's width. With it goes the crash of 5 and 9 October. View → Columns and the saved choice of columns are as they were.
- **The lists aren't built ahead after launch any more** (`warmUp`, and the corner of the window they were built in). Measured on the owner's library: built ahead, the app was busy for 3.8 s of its first half minute, in stalls of up to 0.66 s, just when the first clicks are made; without, 0.7 to 1.0 s. The price is the first opening of a page: Songs and Recently Added 0.4 to 0.6 s once (0.15 s after that), Artists 0.3 to 0.4 s, Downloads 0.35 s. When a list took over a second to build, building ahead was worth it; now it isn't.
- **`Covers.load` named its files as files.** It built each cover's address with a call that, not told whether it's a file or a folder, looks on the disk to find out, on the main thread, twice for every cover not yet in memory: seventy looks for a screenful of songs, 0.4 s of a profile of six searches. It's told now, and nothing is looked up. (The song's own address in the player was built the same way: also told.)
- **A search of the library**, the bar already open, a letter at a time on the owner's library: 0.14 to 0.31 s a change (it was 0.38 to 0.51 s with the new table before this, and 0.69 s with the old one), and typing is never held up by it. A fresh screenful scrolled to: 0.07 to 0.15 s.
- **Putting the window in the Dock and bringing it back (0.6 to 0.75 s) is macOS's own animation**, not the app: a profile shows the main thread inside AppKit's minimise and restore for that long, as it is for any app, and the app's own work in five such moves adding up to 0.7 s. Nothing to mend.
- **The old page arrangement kept for measuring against** (`MUSICORG_PAGES`) is taken out too: its numbers are in the entry of 8 October. `MUSICORG_BENCH` now writes a `launch` line first: how long the app was busy from its start to its first step.
- **The app opened on an empty page for a few minutes of that night** (a build of about 2:36am, mine): SwiftUI sometimes makes the pages' room afresh before it takes the old one apart, and the taking apart put away the page that had already moved to the new room. Until then the lists built ahead had covered for it, by showing the page again a second later; with those gone, the page stayed away until another was chosen. Seen in the first look at the app after the change, and mended: only the pages still in the old room are put away.
- GitHub's checks on the push of 2:17am: everything that ran passed (the secret check, the engine on Windows and the Intel Mac, the app on the Intel Mac); the two jobs on GitHub's other Mac were cancelled for want of a machine, as on 8 October, and were asked for again.

Not timed again in the owner's own window, for the same reason.
- **`MUSICORG_BENCH`'s `heart=` step is a real click now:** the mouse pressed and let go over the heart, through the window (it used to press the button from inside). So the pictures above also show that a click gets to the heart and doesn't select the row.

2026-10-09, about 2:50pm. **A click on a sidebar row counts even when it's the click that brings the app forward, and a ✕ no longer does.** The owner chose it ("1": the harmless things, not ✕ or Remove) from what the entry before this one got wrong.

- **What was wrong.** That entry's notes said a button on a page leaves the click that brings the app forward. Asked properly (of the spot clicked, with the press itself in hand, not with nothing), a button of any kind takes that click on this macOS, and so does a row of tabs. What leaves it is a row of SwiftUI's own list, the sidebar's among them, and a line that's picked by a click or played by a double-click. So with the app behind another, a click on a sidebar row only brought the window forward: the likeliest cause of "sometimes it takes several clicks".
- **`takesFirstClick()`** says it of a view (macOS 15's `allowsWindowActivationEvents`; before that nothing changes). Given to: every sidebar row, which now takes the click itself across its whole width and opens its page (the list picks the row as well when the app is in front); a line of the Downloads page; a video in a channel's or a search's list; a video file under Movies; a song on an artist's page; a search result; a found song in an import. Told `false`: the ✕ on a waiting download, the ✕ on a film being kept, and Stop Keeping. `KNOWN-ISSUES.md` lists what was left as macOS has it, and why.
- **Checked in the unseen copy**, which is never the app in front, so a press sent into it meets macOS's own rule. `MUSICORG_BENCH` learned `first=<x>x<y>` (what the spot would answer), `click=<x>x<y>` (a press and a let-go there) and `state`. Before the change: a sidebar row clicked, and the page stayed; a Downloads line clicked, and it wasn't picked. After: Favourites, Artists and Downloads each opened at one click, the line was picked, the Settings gear (a plain button, untouched) opened Settings as it did before, and the page beside the Downloads lines (a click that's meant to stay as it was) still left it. 2026-10-09, about 7:45pm. **A song or video about to be played is looked up ahead of the line.** The owner's question from the speed audit ("is there any way to increase the speed that it finds and loads a video?"), and their word to carry on without asking.

- Every request to YouTube waits its turn at one rate limiter, a request every second and a half. The turns went to whoever asked first, so a click to play waited behind any batch already asking: What's New is some sixteen look-ups, over twenty seconds of turns. `RateLimiter.wait` and `call` take `first`: a request someone is waiting on takes the next turn, after the one under way. `youtube.stream` and `youtube.video` (the look-ups for playing) say so; the look-up that times a video's lyrics afterwards doesn't.
- **Nothing reaches YouTube sooner or more often:** the gap between requests is the same for all, only the order changes (rule 8 is as it was: one gate, one pace).
- Tests: a request marked `first` goes after the one under way and before the two in line; it waits out the same gap; `stream` and `video` ask that way and `sources` doesn't. Not tried against YouTube: this Mac's look-ups are being kept few while its downloads rest.
- The downloads didn't start again at 3:44pm: the app had been closed at 3:40pm. Eighteen are still waiting, for the next time it's opened.

2026-10-09, about 7:50pm. **A search typed, and an artist's or a channel's page opened, go ahead of the line too.** The same right as a click to play, for the other things someone is looking at the screen waiting for: opening Music Finder starts What's New's batch of look-ups, and a search typed straight after waited behind all of it.

- `youtube.waited_on()` marks the thread that's answering such a call, and every request that thread makes takes the rate limiter's next turn. The engine's call handler puts it round a short list (`rpc.WAITED_ON`: the song search, the video and channel searches, a channel's videos, the artist search and an artist's page, songs and album). Batches aren't on it: suggestions, playlists found, an import's songs.
- The pace is unchanged here as well: the same gap between requests, in another order.
- Tests: the mark holds for its own thread only and comes off afterwards; a marked request goes after the one under way and before the batch in line; the song search is answered under the mark, and no batch call is on the list.

2026-10-09, about 8:30pm. **The app reads the engine's long answers in a tenth of the time, and the pause between downloads holds when the app is reopened.** Carrying on down the speed list without asking, at the owner's word; the second of these was found by a mistake of mine.

- **Opening the app, timed stage by stage** (`MUSICORG_BENCH` now writes `ready`, how long after macOS started the app its library was showing, and a line for each stage of the opening). The engine answers its list of every song in a fifth of a second; the app took a second more to have it. Cause: the app's reader looked through everything that had arrived so far for the end of the answer each time another piece came, and a megabyte comes in dozens of pieces (`RPCConnection.readLoop`). It looks through only the new piece now (`LineBuffer`, tested: lines come out whole however they arrive, and four megabytes in four thousand pieces are read at once).
  - On the made-up library, the unseen copy, old build and new in turn: from the library opened to its songs listed, 0.97 to 1.22 s then 0.27 to 0.40 s; from the app's start to its library showing, 2.9 to 3.2 s then 2.3 to 2.7 s. It's the same gain at every reload of the library (after each download) and for any long answer.
  - In the owner's own app, opened behind their windows: one clean reading each, 4.1 s for the old build and 3.3 s for the new. (The other readings were spoiled: see below.)
  - **Tried and put back:** starting the engine as the app starts, not when its window appears. No gain: what follows waits for the window to be built either way.
- **The pause between downloads was forgotten at every start of the engine**: the first download of a run went at once, whenever the last one had been. Timing the app's opening, I opened and closed the owner's app four times in a minute, and each of the first three fetched a song: three in 24 seconds, against a pace of one every minute or two that the owner had set that morning because the service was refusing this Mac. (The service let all three through, and nothing has been refused since; the fourth opening found the library still held by the third's engine and showed its error until it was closed.) Now a run that has just started counts the pause from this computer's last download, in whichever library: reopened five seconds after a download, it waits out the rest; reopened an hour after, it doesn't wait. Tested both ways.
- The eighteen songs that were waiting are fifteen now.

2026-10-09, about 9:10pm. **The owner's copy of yt-dlp is a nightly build now, and the queue is empty: all eighteen songs that were waiting came down that evening.** Not a change to this repository's code: a difference between the installed library and what these docs assume, noted here as `CLAUDE.md` asks.

- **What was seen.** At the pace the owner set that morning (a song every one to two minutes), with yt-dlp 2026.8.19 (the newest release, of 19 August): downloaded, downloaded, HTTP 403, HTTP 403, downloaded. So the refusals weren't this Mac being shut out: one song is refused and the next let through. The queue reads three refusals in a row as the service objecting, and rests for six hours; that had happened three times since the evening before.
- **What was done.** yt-dlp's own nightly builds are on PyPI as pre-releases, and the newest was five weeks on from the release (2026.9.27.232945.dev0). Installed in the project's `.venv` with `pip install --pre -U "yt-dlp[default]==2026.9.27.232945.dev0"`, the app closed first. The engine's tests pass against it (2,128). Then, at the same pace: six downloads, six through, 80 to 115 seconds apart.
- **To go back:** `.venv/bin/pip install "yt-dlp[default]==2026.8.19"`, with the app closed.
- **How it ended.** With the nightly: twelve songs down in fourteen tries, the two refused being let through when the queue tried them again half an hour on. With the release, earlier that evening: six in eight. So a refusal is of one try, not of a song and not of this Mac, and a later try gets it; whether the nightly is refused less often, these few can't say. No rest was called: there were never three refusals in a row. **What still isn't right:** three refusals in a row read as the service objecting and stop everything for six hours, though each would likely have come through on its next try. That rule is the owner's protection, and hasn't been touched. `pyproject.toml` still asks for plain `yt-dlp[default]`, so GitHub's checks and a fresh install get the release, not this build.

2026-10-10, about 3:20am. A refused download no longer stops everything for six hours at the third: the owner's rule, in their words: "if a song is refused, leave that song alone for 15 minutes and try it again. Move onto another song 1 minute after refusal, if that one is also refused, leave any searches for 30 minutes. Try the 3rd song in a row, if refused wait the 6 hours."

- **The rule, as built** (`queue`): a download refused (HTTP 403) or dropped by the network is tried again 15 minutes later, each time it's refused, up to the five tries any download gets. The queue goes on to a different one a minute after the refusal. If that's refused too (two different downloads in a row, nothing having arrived between them), the whole queue rests for 30 minutes. After the rest a third is tried, one not refused yet if there is one (`QueueStore.next_ready`'s `last`), and only if that's refused as well does the queue pause for six hours. A download that arrives starts the count again.
- **Before:** the refused song was tried again after 1, 5 and 30 minutes and 2 hours, the next song went at the usual pace, and the third different refusal in a row paused everything for six hours with no rest first. On 9 October that had stopped the queue three times, though refusals turned out to be of one try: the same song arrives at the next.
- **Read as:** "leave any searches" is taken to be the queue's own downloads, which is what the six-hour pause stops too. Searching and playing in the app carry on through a rest, as they do through a pause. To be confirmed with the owner.
- **Not changed:** one song refused again and again still counts once (the owner's yes of 8 October), so two songs with no third to try are tried again as their waits end and never rest or pause the queue by themselves. The service saying outright that it's refusing this computer ("confirm you're not a bot", HTTP 429) still pauses for six hours at once. The other retries (1, 5 and 30 minutes, 2 hours) are as they were. The rest is told to the app as the pause is (`paused_by_youtube`, with the time it ends), and `serve` starts the queue again at that time by itself.
- The count of refusals in `queue.sqlite` now keeps each download's job beside it; one kept the old way is still read.

Every comparison of every sort made both its names' forms afresh: some hundreds of thousands of times for 2,000 songs, and all of it twice (the whole library, and the library without the downloads kept apart).
- **Now** each name's form is made once (`Library`, and `sorted(keyed:by:)` in the Kit), then sorted by. The order is the same, and a test holds it to what comparing the names themselves gives, on names chosen to trip it (accents, capitals, "The", the same title by two artists).

So from that page it's one file at a drag, even with several lines picked.

2026-10-10, about 2:45pm. "Delete from Library" is built, as the owner chose it: the song leaves the library and its file is kept, set aside.

- **The owner's answer** to what becomes of the file: "Set it aside". So a download deleted from the library moves to the library's own `_Replaced/` folder, at the same place under it that it had under `Music/`, with its `.lrc`, and the album's `cover.jpg` with the album's last song. Nothing there is ever deleted by the engine.
- **Engine:** the `remove` plan takes `keep` (`plan.create`'s options; `pipeline.plan_remove`). With it each file is moved by `fileops.supersede`, the move that already sets a replaced file aside, where without it each goes to the Trash. Either way the song leaves the index, the favourites, the play counts and the playlists, and can be downloaded again. Journaled; **undo moves the files back** to `Music/` and the library lists the song again, though its place in a playlist or among the favourites isn't put back. A plan made before today has no `keep` and goes to the Trash as it was made to. Downloads only, as before: a song from the owner's own files is refused either way.
- **Contract:** `docs/LIBRARY_CONTRACT.md` now says `_Replaced/` also holds downloads the owner took out of the library but kept (the folder's line, and rule 6.5), and `CLAUDE.md`'s rule 4 says the same. No new folder and no new kind of write: the move is one the engine already made.
- **App:** a download's right-click menu has "Delete from Library…" above "Delete from Computer…", each with its own question first. The first says the file stays on this Mac, in the library folder's "_Replaced" folder. There's no button yet to put such a song back: `musicorg undo <batch>` does it, or the file can be moved back by hand and the library rebuilt.
- **Checked:** the engine's tests (the file, its lyrics and cover in `_Replaced/` byte for byte, nothing in the Trash, gone from the index and the favourites, downloadable again, undo putting all three back and the library listing it again; a plan without `keep` still goes to the Trash); and through the app itself, in the unseen copy on the made-up library (`MUSICORG_BENCH`'s `remove=library`): the newest download left the lists (95 downloads, then 94), its file was in `_Replaced/`, and `undo` brought it back (95). **Not tried by hand:** the menu item and its question in the app in front.

2026-10-10, about 3:15pm. Settings → Deleted Items: the downloads deleted from the library but not from the computer, kept as a log, each with a button to undo it. The owner's ask: "In settings, show a list. Deleted items that weren't removed from computer. And it shows it, keep it as a log, and has a button to undo each individual song."

- **Engine:** `library.removed` reads the log from the journal (`pipeline.removed`): every download a `remove` plan with `keep` set aside, newest first, with its title and artist (from the file's tags wherever it is now, or from the name it had), where it was, when, and how it stands: `kept` (it can be put back), `restored` (it has been) or `missing` (its file is no longer in `_Replaced/`: moved or deleted by hand). Nothing is written, and no new record is kept: the journal already is one. `library.put_back` undoes one such batch, here and now, and answers with the log as it then is; any other kind of batch is refused. The three states are in `docs/ENGINE_API.md`'s enums.
- **One song, one undo:** the app now deletes from the library one batch a song (`AppModel.setAside`), so each can be put back by itself. A batch that set several aside (made from the command line, say) is put back whole, and its button says how many.
- **App:** a new section of Settings, Deleted Items, last in the list, laid out as the other sections' lists are (no drawing was asked for: say if it should stand otherwise). A line is the song's title, its artist and the day it was deleted, with Undo at its end; once put back the line stays, and says "Put back" and the day in the button's place.
- **What undo does and doesn't:** the file goes back where it was under `Music/` with its lyrics (and the album's cover), and the library lists the song again. It isn't put back into the playlists or favourites it left. If the same song has been downloaded again in the meantime, the one put back comes in beside it with " (2)" in its name: nothing is overwritten.
- **Checked:** the engine's tests (the log lists only what was kept, not what went to the Trash; a song put back is in the library's list again and still in the log; one whose kept file was taken away says so; nothing but a kept delete can be put back). And through the app, in the unseen copy on the made-up library: a download deleted from the library was listed with Undo, Undo put it back (95 downloads again) and the line read "Put back 10 Oct 2026". **Not clicked by hand** in the app in front.

2026-10-10, about 4:10pm. What's done on a phone player comes back at a sync, and a song's listening time. The owner's asks: "The phone app should allow the user to hold a song and an option comes up, move to playlist - This would inturn when synced with the app, grab data, and changes made on the phone"; a playlist can be made on the phone too ("yes, you can create one on the phone"); and "It would be cool if there was a way to track the amount of minutes a song is played, not completed song plays, but, 'this song has been listened to 255 minutes 24 seconds.'"

- **A promise changed, at the owner's request.** Until today the home share took nothing from a phone: "No request changes anything on the computer". Now a paired phone may tell the computer three things that were done on it, and nothing else: **a playlist made, a song put in a playlist, and time spent listening to a song**. What the promise still says, word for word in `docs/ENGINE_API.md` (section 3) and `CLAUDE.md`: a phone never changes, adds or removes a music file, a tag, a cover or lyrics, and never touches a file in the library's music folders. What it can change is what `listening` keeps in `state.json`: the playlists, and each song's listening time. It can't rename or delete a playlist, take a song out of one, or change a favourite or a play count.
- **The request** (`sharing`, format still 1): `GET /sync/v1/hello` says `"accepts": ["changes"]`, and `POST /sync/v1/changes` with the device's key takes `{ "changes": [...] }` and answers `{ "taken": [ids] }`. The three kinds are a new enum, **Sync change kind**: `playlist_new`, `playlist_add`, `listened`. The list's ids are hashes, so `sharing` finds its way back from one to the library's own id for a song or a playlist, and hands `listening` the changes in the library's terms; it writes nothing itself.
- **Each change is taken once.** A change can arrive twice when its answer was lost. A short hash of every change taken is kept in `state.json` beside what the changes changed, saved in the same write, so a second arrival does nothing, also after the app has been closed and opened again. The newest 5,000 are remembered. The same record says which of the library's playlists was made for a playlist the phone made, so a song put in "its" playlist in a later request lands in ours.
- **Decided while building** (say if any should be otherwise):
  - **A playlist made on the phone under a name there already is: the one there is, is used.** The phone can't see a playlist made on the Mac since it last synced, and two of one name couldn't be told apart. Capitals don't count ("driving" lands in "Driving").
  - **A song the playlist already has isn't put in a second time.** For the same reason: the phone can't see what was put in on the Mac meanwhile. (On the Mac a song can be in a playlist twice, and the app asks first.) The agreement's own words are "the song goes at the end"; this is the one place the engine does less than it was told.
  - **One figure for a song.** Listening time from the phone and from the Mac's own player go into the same number (`listening.listened`), and the list gives it back as `listened` on each track, for the phone to show.
  - **A change that can never be done is answered as dealt with**, so the phone lets go of it: the song or playlist is gone, or the change makes no sense. **A kind this engine doesn't know is left out of the answer**, so a newer phone keeps it for a newer engine.
- **Limits, so nothing a phone sends can damage the library's records:** a body of a megabyte at most (more is refused whole, unread: 413); the first 2,000 changes of a request (the rest wait for the next sync); a day of listening at most in one change; a name cut to the 200 characters the app allows; no new playlist in a library that has 1,000, and no song into a playlist that has 10,000. Of what a phone sends, only a playlist's name is kept as it came: the ids it makes up are kept as short hashes. Nothing of it is logged but counts.
- **The same guards as every request:** a caller at home, the `Host` and `Origin` checks, and the device's key. A device that was unpaired is refused here too. An engine that doesn't hold the library's lock says `"accepts": []` and takes nothing.
- **Engine, for the app:** `listening.listened` (`{ track_id, seconds }` → the song's total), `listened` in `listening.get`, and a notification, `listening.changed`, when a phone's changes changed a playlist or a time. `listening.forget` drops a deleted download's time with its play count.
- **The Mac app counts too**, so the figure is everything the owner has listened to, not only the phone's share: the player counts the time a song was really playing (a jump along the bar isn't listening; `PlayedTime` in the Kit) and tells the engine when the song ends, is paused or gives way to another, and as the app closes (`RPCConnection.tell`, sent ahead of the engine being told to stop). A song played from the service has no id in the library and isn't counted. **It starts from today:** nothing is worked out from the plays counted before.
- **Where it's seen:** a new column in the song lists, **Listened** ("255 min 24 sec"), which can be sorted by. It's **hidden until switched on** in View → Columns (or a right-click on the headings), so no list looks different until the owner asks. A playlist made on the phone appears in the sidebar when the phone syncs. The note under the switch in Settings → Sync no longer says the phone can only read: it says a music file can't be changed, and names the three things that come back.
- Not built: favourites and play counts coming back from the phone, and any other change to a playlist (a rename, a song taken out, a new order). The Artists page's "Time listening" is still worked out from plays; it could use the real figure once there's some. The choices made here that are the owner's to change are in `docs/KNOWN-ISSUES.md`.
- **Checked:** the engine's tests, all of them (2,152 pass, 5 skipped as ever), and `ruff`. The new ones ask the share over real HTTP on this computer's own loopback address: a change sent twice is applied once, also after the share was stopped and started again; a song put in a phone-made playlist by a later request lands in it; a kind not known is left with the phone; a song, video or playlist that isn't there, and every kind of nonsense where a length of time should be, is dropped and answered as dealt with; no key, a wrong key, an unpaired device's key and a web page's request are refused; a body that isn't JSON, or is too long, is turned away and `state.json` isn't so much as written again; every file in the library but `state.json` is as it was, with nothing journaled; and nothing of a phone's ids is kept or logged but hashes. The app's tests (127).
- **Looked at in the app,** in the unseen copy on a made-up library of silent songs, with a made-up paired phone asking this branch's engine over loopback only (that share listened on no other address and wasn't announced): `hello` said `accepts: ["changes"]`; a playlist made on the "phone" appeared in the sidebar while the app was open; one made as "made on the mac" went into "Made On The Mac" and made no second; 125.5 seconds listened showed as "2 min 5 sec"; the same changes sent again changed nothing; a kind not known was left out of the answer. The app's own player, with two songs played, one skipped to the next, paused, played again and the app quit: 7.6 and 13 seconds kept, the last six of them sent as the app closed, and no play count moved. The default song list, pictured with the table as it was and as it is: the same, dot for dot.
- **Not tried:** a real phone; the owner's own library; the Listened column switched on by hand from View → Columns (the unseen copy switched it on through the same menu item); and how the list reads with thousands of songs' times in it.

2026-10-10, about 7:15pm. **The Mac stays awake while a phone syncs.** The third thing the phone's wish-list needed from the computer, after changes coming back and listening time: a film takes minutes to copy, and a Mac that went to sleep half-way cut the sync off.

- **Engine** (`sharing`, `Share.busy`): a paired device's request for the list or a file, or its changes, keeps the computer from going to sleep, and so does every piece of a file on its way. It's let go when the device has asked for nothing for two minutes, and at once when sharing is switched off or the app closes. The means is the one the queue has used since step 09a (`tools.keep_awake`: `caffeinate -i` on a Mac, which ends by itself if the engine does; the screen may still turn off).
- **Only a paired device counts:** `hello`, a pairing code and a request with no key or a wrong one keep nothing awake, so nobody else at home can hold the computer up.
- **What it doesn't do:** wake a Mac that's already asleep. Whether a phone's request can wake it depends on macOS's "Wake for network access" and on something at home that answers for a sleeping Mac (an Apple TV does); that wasn't tried.
- **App:** the note in Settings → Sync says the Mac stays awake while a device is syncing. No switch for it: say if there should be one.
- Checked: the engine's tests (a new one: nothing keeps the computer awake before a paired device asks, each file asked for puts the moment off, quiet lets go, the next sync holds it again, and switching sharing off lets go at once), and once for real on this Mac, on loopback with the made-up library: `caffeinate` was running just after the list was asked for, gone after the quiet time, back at the next request, and gone when sharing was switched off. **Not tried:** a real phone's sync of a film with the Mac left alone long enough to have slept.

Hidden, a row carries no time at all, so a time that changes makes no row different and nothing is worked out or drawn again. Switching the column on works the list out once, with the times as they are.

The app's player now makes two at a song's end where it made one, and one at a pause or a skip. They're made by the engine, not the app, so nothing on screen waits for them.

2026-10-10, about 7:35pm. **A skip no longer makes the next song's lyrics wait for a save.** What the measuring above turned up: the app told the engine a song's listening time at the very moment of a skip or a pause, the engine saved the library's records for it (a tenth of a second) and answers the app's requests in turn, so the next song's lyrics, asked for in the same moment, waited behind that save. Before listening time was kept, a skip saved nothing.

Whatever it holds is told at once, without waiting for answers, before the engine is stopped (the app closing, a change of profile) and before another library is opened, so the time is kept, and kept with the library it was listened in.

  - **an id of its own making** (`"id"` in the body: random, made once by the phone, the same each time). This is what covers the real case, a phone that forgot the library and its key with it. Only the id's SHA-256 is kept, in `devices.json` beside the key's; it's never logged, and the app is never given it.

- A device already listed twice becomes one line the next time it pairs, if it's known both ways; otherwise the owner removes the stale line with Remove, as now. Nothing is removed by itself.

2026-10-11, about 5:15pm. **A playlist's songs dragged into order, and a playlist saved as a file.** The third of the three, and the last two things on the roadmap's v0.2 list for playlists.

- **Drag to reorder.** In a playlist, a song (or several picked together) is dragged to another place in the list and stays picked where it lands. It works while the list is in the playlist's own order: sorted by a column or searched, the lines on screen aren't the playlist's order, a drop has no place to mean, and none is taken (Move Up and Move Down in the right-click menu already went by the same rule, and are still there). No other list takes a drop: only a playlist has an order of the owner's.
  - It's the playlist's record that changes (`playlist.set_tracks`, as Move Up does); no file moves. A row dragged out of the app is still the song's own file, to copy.
  - `SongTable` takes the drop (`reorder`), `SongList` says when a list may (`ordered`), and the reckoning of the new order is in the Kit (`Playlist.moving`), tested there.
